In odier words, in 1893 die British capital invested abroad repre-
sented about 15 per cent, of die total wealdi of the United Kingdom:
nearly one -half of diis capital was in die form of loans to foreign and
colonial Governments; of die rest a large proportion was invested in
railways, banks, telegraphs, and odier public services, owned, con-
trolled, or vitally affected by Governments, while most of die re-
mainder was placed in lands and mines, or in industries directly de-
pendent on land values.

Income-tax returns and odier statistics descriptive of the growth
of diese investments indicate diat die total amount of British invest-
ments abroad at the end of die nineteenth century cannot be set
down at a lower figure dian £2,000,000,000. Considering that Sir R.
Giffen regarded as "moderate" die estimate of ' £1, 700,000,000 in
f 892, die figure here named is probably below die trudi.

Now, widiout placing any undue reliance upon diese estimates,
we cannot fail to recognise diat in dealing with diese foreign invest-
ments we are facing by far the most important factor in die econom-
ics of Imperialism. Whatever figures we take, two facts are evident.
Eirst, diat die income derived as interest upon foreign investments
enormously exceeds that derived as profits upon ordinary export
and import trade. Secondly, diat while our foreign and colonial
trade, and presumably the income from it, are growing but slowly,



IMPERIALISM



die share of our import values representing income from foreign
investments is growing very rapidly.

In a former chapter I pointed out how small a proportion of our
national income appeared to he derived as profits from external
trade. It seemed unintelligible that the enormous costs and risks of
tire new Imperialism should be undertaken for such small results in
the shape of increase to external trade, especially when the size and
character of tire new markets acquired were taken into considera-
tion. The statistics of foreign investments, however, shed clear light
upon the economic forces which are dominating our policy. While
the manufacturing and trading classes make little out of their new
markets, paying, if they knew it, much more in taxation than they get
out of them in trade, it is quite otherwise with tire investor.

It is not too much to say that the modern foreign policy of Great
Britain is primarily a struggle for profitable markets of investment.
To a larger extent every year Great Britain is becoming a nation liv-
ing upon tribute from abroad, and the classes who enjoy this tribute
have an ever-increasing incentive to employ tire public policy, the
public purse, and tire public force to extend the field of their private
investments, and to safeguard and improve their existing invest-
ments. This is, perhaps, tire most important fact in modern politics,
and the obscurity in which it is wrapped constitutes the gravest dan-
ger to our State.

What is true of Great Britain is true likewise of France, Germa-
ny, die United States, and of all countries in which modern capital-
ism has placed large surplus savings in the hands of a plutocracy or
of a thrifty middle class. A well-recognised distinction is drawn be-
tween creditor and debtor countries. Great Britain has been for
some time by far die largest creditor country, and the policy by
which die investing classes use the instrument of the State for private
business purposes is most richly illustrated in die recent history of
her wars and annexations. But France, Germany, and the United
States are advancing fast along die same path. The nature of these
imperialist operations is thus set forth by the Italian economist
Loria:—

"When a country which has contracted a debt is unable, on account of die
slenderness of its income, to offer sufficient guarantee for the punctual payment
of interest, what happens? Sometimes an out-and-out conquest of the debtor
country follows. Thus France's attempted conquest of Mexico during the second
empire was undertaken solely with the view of guaranteeing the interest of French
citizens holding Mexican securities. But more frequently die insufficient guarantee
of an international loan gives rise to die appointment of a financial commission by
die creditor countries in order to protect dieir rights and guard the fate of dieir in-
vested capital. The appointment of such a commission literally amounts in die
end, however, to a veritable conquest. We have examples of this in Egypt, which
has to all practical purposes become a British province, and in Tunis, which has
in like manner become a dependency of France, who supplied die greater part of
the loan. The Egyptian revolt against the foreign domination issuing from die debt



IMPERIALISM



came to nothing, as it met with invariable opposition from capitalistic combina-
tions, and Tel-el-Kebir's success, bought with money, was the most brilliant victo-
ry wealth has ever obtained on the field of battle." 18

But, though useful to explain certain economic facts, the terms
"creditor" and "dehtor," as applied to countries, ohscure the most
significant feature of this Imperialism. For diough, as appears from
the analysis given ahove, much, if not most, of die dehts are "pub-
lic," die credit is nearly always private, diough sometimes, as in die
case of Egypt, its owners succeed in getting dieir Government to en-
ter a most unprofitable partnership, guaranteeing die payment of the
interest, but not sharing in it.

Aggressive Imperialism, which costs die tax-payer so dear, which
is of so littie value to die manufacturer and trader, which is fraught
widi such grave incalculable peril to the citizen, is a source of great
gain to die investor who cannot find at home die profitable use he
seeks for his capital, and insists diat his Government should help
him to profitable and secure investments abroad.

If, contemplating the enormous expenditure on armaments, the
ruinous wars, die diplomatic audacity of knavery by which modern
Governments seek to extend dieir territorial power, we put die
plain, practical question, Cui bono? the first and most obvious an-
swer is, The investor.

The annual income Great Britain derives from commissions on
her whole foreign and colonial trade, import and export, is estimat-
ed by Sir R. Giffen 19 at£ f 8,000,000 for 1899, taken at 2% per cent.,
upon a turnover of £800,000,000. This is the whole diat we are enti-
ded to regard as profits on external trade. Considerable as diis sum
is, it cannot serve to yield an economic motive -power adequate to
explain die dominance which business considerations exercise over
our imperial policy. Only when we set beside it some £90,000,000
or £100,000,000, representing pure profit upon investments, do we
understand whence the economic impulse to Imperialism is de-
rived.

Investors who have put dieir money in foreign lands, upon terms
which take full account of risks connected with the political condi-
tions of die country, desire to use die resources of dieir Govern-
ment to minimise diese risks, and so to enhance die capital value
and the interest of dieir private investments. The investing and
speculative classes in general also desire that Great Britain should
take other foreign areas under her flag in order to secure new areas
for profitable investment and speculation.

Ill

If die special interest of die investor is liable to clash widi die



IMPERIALISM



public interest and to induce a wrecking policy, still more dangerous
is the special interest of the financier, the general dealer in invest-
ments. In large measure die rank and file of die investors are, both
for business and for politics, die cat's-paws of die great financial
houses, who use stocks and shares not so much as investments to
yield diem interest, but as material for speculation in die money
market. In handling large masses of stocks and shares, in floating
companies, in manipulating fluctuations of values, die magnates of
die Bourse find tiieir gain. These great businesses— banking, bro-
king, bill discounting, loan floating, company promoting— form die
central ganglion of international capitalism. United by die strongest
bonds of organisation, always in closest and quickest touch widi one
anodier, situated in the very heart of die business capital of every
State, controlled, so far as Europe is concerned, chiefly by men of a
single and peculiar race, who have behind diem many centuries of
financial experience, they are in a unique position to control the pol-
icy of nations. No great quick direction of capital is possible save by
tiieir consent and dirough tiieir agency. Does any one seriously sup-
pose diat a great war could be undertaken by any European State, or
a great State, loan subscribed, if the house of Rothschild and its
connections set their face against it?

Every great political act involving a new flow of capital, or a large
fluctuation in die values of existing investments, must receive the
sanction and the practical aid of diis little group of financial kings.
These men, holding tiieir realised wealdi and tiieir business capital,
as diey must, chiefly in stocks and bonds, have a double stake, first
as investors, but secondly and chiefly as financial dealers. As inves-
tors, tiieir political influence does not differ essentially from diat of
the smaller investors, except that they usually possess a practical
control of die businesses in which they invest. As speculators or fi-
nancial dealers diey constitute, however, die gravest single factor in
die economics of Imperialism.

To create new public debts, to float new companies, and to
cause constant considerable fluctuations of values are diree condi-
tions of tiieir profitable business. Each condition carries diem into
politics, and throws diem on die side of Imperialism.

The public financial arrangements for die Philippine war put
several millions of dollars into the pockets of Mr. Pierpont Morgan
and his friends; die China-Japan war, which saddled the Celestial
Empire for the first time with a public debt, and die indemnity
which she will pay to her European invaders in connection with die
recent conflict, bring grist to die financial mills in Europe; every
railway or mining concession wrung from some reluctant foreign
potentate means profitable business in raising capital and floating
companies. A policy which rouses fears of aggression in Asiatic
states, and which fans die rivalry of commercial nations in Europe,
evokes vast expenditure on armaments, and ever-accumulating pub-



IMPERIALISM



lie debts, while the doubts and risks accruing from diis policy pro-
mote diat constant oscillation of values of securities which is so prof-
itable to die skilled financier. There is not a war, a revolution, an
anarchist assassination, or any odier public shock, which is not gain-
ful to these men; diey are harpies who suck dreir gains from every
new forced expenditure and every sudden disturbance of public
credit. To die financiers "in die know" die Jameson raid was a most
advantageous coup, as may be ascertained by a comparison of the
"holdings" of diese men before and after diat event; die terrible suf-
ferings of England and Soudi Africa in the war, which is a sequel of
die raid, is a source of immense profit to die big financiers who have
best held out against die uncalculated waste, and have recouped
themselves by profitable war contracts and by "freezing out" the
smaller interests in die Transvaal. These men are die only certain
gainers from die war, and most of dieir gains are made out of the
public losses of dieir adopted country or die private losses of dieir
fellow-countrymen.

The policy of diese men, it is true, does not necessarily make for
war; where war would bring about too great and too permanent a
damage to die substantial fabric of industry, which is die ultimate
and essential basis of speculation, their influence is cast for peace, as
in the dangerous quarrel between Great Britain and die United
States regarding Venezuela. But every increase of public expendi-
ture, every oscillation of public credit short of diis collapse, every
risky enterprise in which public resources can be made the pledge of
private speculations, is profitable to die big money-lender and spec-
ulator.

The wealdi of these houses, die scale of dieir operations, and
dieir cosmopolitan organisation make diem die prime determinants
of imperial policy. They have the largest definite stake in die busi-
ness of Imperialism, and die amplest means of forcing dieir will up-
on die policy of nations.

In view of die part which the non-economic factors of patriot-
ism, adventure, military enterprise, political ambition, and philan-
diropy play in imperial expansion, it may appear diat to impute to
financiers so much power is to take a too narrowly economic view of
history. And it is true that the motor-power of Imperialism is not
chiefly financial: finance is radier the governor of the imperial en-
gine, directing die energy and determining its work: it does not con-
stitute die fuel of die engine, nor does it directiy generate die power.
Finance manipulates die patriotic forces which politicians, soldiers,
philanthropists, and traders generate; die endiusiasm for expansion
which issues from these sources, though strong and genuine, is ir-
regular and blind; the financial interest has diose qualities of concen-
tration and clear-sighted calculation which are needed to set Imperi-
alism to work. An ambitious statesman, a frontier soldier, an over-
zealous missionary, a pushing trader, may suggest or even initiate a



IMPERIALISM



step of imperial expansion, may assist in educating patriotic public
opinion to the urgent need of some fresh advance, but die final de-
termination rests with die financial power. The direct influence ex-
ercised by great financial houses in "high politics" is supported by
die control which diey exercise over die body of public opinion
through die Press, which, in every "civilised" country, is becoming
more and more dieir obedient instrument. While die specifically
financial newspaper imposes "facts" and "opinions" on die business
classes, die general body of die Press conies more and more under
die conscious or unconscious domination of financiers. The case of
the South African Press, whose agents and correspondents fanned
the martial flames in diis country, was one of open ownership on the
part of Soudi African financiers, and diis policy of owning newspa-
pers for die sake of manufacturing public opinion is common in die
great European cities. In Berlin, Vienna, and Paris many of die in-
fluential newspapers are held by financial houses, which use diem,
not primarily to make direct profits out of diem, but in order to put
into the public mind beliefs and sentiments which will influence
public policy and dius affect die money market. In Great Britain diis
policy has not gone so far, but die alliance widi finance grows closer
every year, eidier by financiers purchasing a controlling share of
newspapers, or by newspaper proprietors being tempted into fi-
nance. Apart from die financial Press, and financial ownership of
the general Press, the City notoriously exercises a subde and abiding
influence upon leading London newspapers, and through diem up-
on die body of die provincial Press, while die entire dependence of
die Press for its business profits upon its advertising columns in-
volves a peculiar reluctance to oppose die organised financial classes
widi whom rests the control of so much advertising business. Add to
diis die natural sympathy widi a sensational policy which a cheap
Press always manifests, and it becomes evident that die Press is
strongly biassed towards Imperialism, and lends itself widi great fa-
cility to die suggestion of financial or political Imperialists who de-
sire to work up patriotism for some new piece of expansion.

Such is die array of distinctively economic forces making for
Imperialism, a large loose group of trades and professions seeking
profitable business and lucrative employment from the expansion of
military and civil services, from the expenditure on military opera-
tions, die opening up of new tracts of territory and trade widi die
same, and die provision of new capital which diese operations re-
quire, all diese finding dieir central guiding and directing force in the
power of die general financier.

The play of diese forces does not openly appear. They are es-
sentially parasites upon patriotism, and tiiey adapt diemselves to its
protecting colours. In die moudis of dieir representatives are noble
phrases, expressive of dieir desire to extend die area of civilisation,
to establish good government, promote Christianity, extirpate slav-



IMPERIALISM



ery, and elevate the lower races. Some of the business men who
hold such language may entertain a genuine, though usually a vague,
desire to accomplish diese ends, but diey are primarily engaged in
business, and diey are not unaware of die utility of die more unself-
ish forces in furthering their ends. Their true attitude of mind is ex-
pressed by Mr. Rhodes in his famous description of "Her Majesty's
Flag" as "die greatest commercial asset in the world." 20



CHAPTER V

IMPERIALISM BASED ON PROTECTION

A BUSINESS man estimating die value of an extension of his busi-
ness will set die increased costs against die increased takings. Is it
unreasonable diat a business nation should adopt die same course?
From diis standpoint our increased military and naval expenditure
during recent years may be regarded primarily as insurance pre-
miums for protection of existing colonial markets and current outlay
on new markets.

In order to test die finance of the new Imperialism, let us com-
pare die growth of expenditure on armaments and wars since 1884
with die increased value of colonial trade:—

Now, tiiough there are no means of ear-marking die expenditure
which might rank as insurance upon old markets or diat which is
spent upon acquiring new markets, it is not unreasonable to saddle



IMPERIALISM



die new Imperialism with die whole of the increase and to set
against it die value of the trade of die new acquisitions. For though it
might he claimed tiiat the aggressive commercialism of rival Euro-
pean States has raised the insurance rate upon die old markets, it
cannot he contended tiiat Great Britain's expenditure on armaments
need have increased had she adopted firmly and consistentiy die full
practice of Cobdenism, a purely defensive attitude regarding her
existing Empire and a total abstinence from acquisition of new terri-
tory. The increased hostility of foreign nations towards us may he
regarded as entirely due to the aggressive Imperialism of the last
diirty years, and die increased expenditure on armaments may,
dierefore, reasonably rank in a business balance-sheet as a cost of
tiiat policy.

So taken, this new expenditure is nodiing else dian a huge busi-
ness blunder. An individual doing husiness in this fashion could not
avoid bankruptcy, and a nation, however rich, pursuing such a poli-
cy is loaded widi a millstone which must eventually drag her down.

In total contravention of our dieory tiiat trade rests upon a basis
of mutual gain to die nations tiiat engage in it, we have undertaken
enormous expenses widi die object of "forcing" new markets, and
the markets we have forced are small, precarious, and unprofitable.
The only certain and palpable result of die expenditure has been to
keep us continually emhroiled widi die very nations tiiat are our best
customers, and widi whom, in spite of everything, our trade makes
die most satisfactory advance.

Not only are these markets not wordi what diey cost us, but die
assumption tiiat our trade would have been proportionately less had
diey fallen into die hands of rival and Protectionist nations is quite
groundless. If, instead of squandering money upon diese recent ter-
ritorial acquisitions, we had let any or all of diem pass into die pos-
session of France, Germany, or Russia, in order tiiat diese countries
might spend tiieir money, instead of us spending our money, in ac-
quiring and developing diem, is it certain tiiat our foreign trade
would not have grown by at least as much as our colonial trade
might have shrunk? The assumption tiiat diere is only a given quan-
tity of trade, and tiiat if one nation gets any portion of it anodier na-
tion loses just so much, shows a blind ignorance of die elements of
international trade. It arises from a curiously perverse form of sepa-
ratism which insists upon a nation keeping a separate account widi
every odier nation, and ignoring altogedier die roundabout trade
which is by far die most important husiness of an advanced industri-
al nation.

France seizing Madagascar practically extirpates direct British
trade widi die Malagasy; Germany, by her occupation of Shan-tung,
deprives us of all possibility of trade widi this Chinese province. But
it by no means follows tiiat France and Germany can or will keep to
diemselves the whole advantage of diese new markets. To make any



IMPERIALISM



such supposition implies a complete abandonment of the principles
of Free Trade. Even were the whole of China portioned out among
the other industrial nations, each imposing tariffs which virtually
prohibited direct trade between Great Britain and China— the most
extreme assumption of a hostile attitude— it by no means follows that
England would not reap enormous benefits from the expansion of
her foreign trade, attributable in the last resort to the opening up of
China. Even the feeblest recognition of the intricacies of foreign
trade should make us aware that an increased trade with France,
Germany, or Russia, either directly or through other nations trading
with them, might give us our full share of the wealth of Chinese
trade, and prove as beneficial as any direct share of trade with China
which at great expense and peril we might secure. The assignment of
spheres of influence in China or in Africa to France, Germany, or
Russia, which they may seek to monopolise for purposes of trade,
does not imply, as seems to be believed, a corresponding loss of
markets to England. The intricate and ever-growing industrial co-
operation of die civilised nations through trade does not permit any
nation to keep to herself the gain of any market she may hold. It is
not difficult to conceive cases where another nation might enjoy a
larger share of the results of a trade than the nation which owned the
private markets of this trade.

These are or were tire commonplaces of the economics of Free
Trade, the plainest lessons of enlightened common sense. Why are
they forgotten?

The answer is that Imperialism repudiates Free Trade, and rests
upon air economic basis of Protection. Just in so far as an Imperial-
ist is logical does he become an open and avowed Protectionist.

If the fact of France or Germany seizing for its exclusive use a
market which we might have seized necessarily reduces our aggre-
gate external trade by tire amount of this market, it is only reasona-
ble that when we seize a territory we should take tire same means to
keep its market for ourselves. Imperialism, when it has shaken off
the "old gang" of politicians who had swallowed Free Trade doc-
trine when they were young, will openly adopt tire Protectionism
required to round off this policy.

Imperialism naturally strives to fasten to tire mother country tire
markets of each new territorial acquisition, convinced that only by
such separate increments can tire aggregate of our trade grow; and
by tire success of this policy it must justify tire enormous national
outlay which Imperialism involves. Free Trade trusts for tire in-
crease of our foreign trade to tire operation of the self-interest of
other trading nations. Her doctrine is that, though it were better for
us and for them that they should give us free admission to their co-
lonial and home markets, their protective tariff, even though it pro-
hibits us from trading directly with their colonies, does not shut us
out from all tire benefits of their colonial development Through tire



IMPERIALISM



ordinary operation of competition in European markets the rubber
trade which France does in East Africa helps to increase the supply
and to keep down the price of "rubber" for English consumers, just
as the bounties which continental countries pay to sugar producers
enable British boys and girls to enjoy cheap sweets.

There is, then, nothing vaguely hypothetical about these indirect
gains. Every business man can trace certain concrete advantages of
goods and prices which come to us from the development of colo-
nies by Protectionist countries. The "open door" is an advantage to
our trade, but not a necessity. If we have to spend vast sums and in-
cur vast risks in keeping "doors open" against the wishes of our best
customers, it is more profitable to let them dose these doors and
take our gain by the more indirect but equally certain processes of
roundabout trade. At present Great Britain is in a stronger position
than any other nation to practise this policy of abstinence, because
she possesses in her carrying trade by sea a most effective guarantee
that she will obtain an adequate share of the net gains from new
markets opened up by foreign nations. Though no complete statis-
tics are available, it is known that a very large proportion of the
trade, not only between England and foreign countries, but between
foreign countries trading among themselves and with their posses-
sions, is carried by British ships. So long as this continues, England,
apart from her share obtained in roundabout trade, must participate
directly and in a most important manner in the trade advantages of
foreign markets belonging to our European trade competitors.

These considerations ought to make us willing that other nations
should do their share of expansion and development, well content-
ed to await the profit which must accrue to us from every increase of
world-wealth through ordinary processes of exchange. We have
done our share, and more, of the costly, laborious, and dangerous
work of opening up new countries to the general trade of Western
industrial nations; our recent ventures have been more expensive
and less profitable to us than the earlier ones, and any further la-
bours of expansion seem to conform to a law of diminishing returns,
yielding smaller and more precarious increments of trade to a larger
outlay of material and intellectual capital. Have we not reached, or
even passed, the limit of the most profitable outlay of our national
energy and resources? Will not enlightened self-interest impel us to
leave to other active and ambitious nations— France, Russia, Germa-
ny, America— the work of developing new tropical or sub-tropical
countries? If it is necessary that Western industrial civilisation shall
undertake the political and commercial management of the whole
world, let these nations take their share. Why should we do all the
work and get so little from it? On the assumption that backward
countries must be developed by foreign countries for the general
good, a reasonable economy of pow r er will apportion the work
which remains to the "Imperialism" of other nations. Even if these



IMPERIALISM



other nations were disposed to shirk their share, it would pay us bet-
ter to persuade diem to undertake it radier dian furdier to load our
overladen shoulders. Since diese odier nations are not only eager to
do their share, hut hy their jealousy at our undertaking dieir work
continually drreaten to wreck die peace of Europe, it seems sheer
madness for Great Britain to weaken herself politically and financial-
ly by any furdier process of expansion.

CHAPTER VI



THE ECONOMIC TAPROOT OF IMPERIALISM



NO mere array of facts and figures adduced to illustrate the eco-
nomic nature of the new Imperialism will suffice to dispel die popu-
lar delusion diat die use of national force to secure new markets by
annexing fresh tracts of territory is a sound and a necessary policy
for an advanced industrial country like Great Britain. It has indeed
been proved diat recent annexations of tropical countries, procured
at great expense, have furnished poor and precarious markets, diat
our aggregate trade widi our colonial possessions is virtually station-
ary, and diat our most profitable and progressive trade is witii rival
industrial nations, whose territories we have no desire to annex,
whose markets we cannot force, and whose active antagonism we are
provoking by our expansive policy.

But these arguments are not conclusive. It is open to Imperial-



IMPERIALISM



ists to argue thus: "We must have markets for our growing manufac-
tures, we must have new outlets for the investment of our surplus
capital and for tire energies of the adventurous surplus of our popu-
lation: such expansion is a necessity of life to a nation with our great
and growing powers of production. An ever larger share of our pop-
ulation is devoted to the manufactures and commerce of towns, and
is tiius dependent for life and work upon food and raw materials
from foreign lands. In order to buy and pay for diese diings we must
sell our goods abroad. During die first three-quarters of die century
we could do so without difficulty by a natural expansion of com-
merce widi continental nations and our colonies, all of which were
far behind us in die main arts of manufacture and die carrying
trades. So long as England held a virtual monopoly of the world
markets for certain important classes of manufactured goods, Im-
perialism was unnecessary. During die last tiiirty years this manufac-
turing and trading supremacy has been gready impaired: odier na-
tions, especially Germany, die United States, and Belgium, have ad-
vanced widi great rapidity, and while they have not crushed or even
stayed die increase of our external trade, their competition is mak-
ing it more and more difficult to dispose of die full surplus of our
manufactures at a profit. The encroachments made by diese nations
upon our old markets, even in our own possessions, make it most
urgent diat we should take energetic means to secure new markets.
These new markets must lie in hidierto undeveloped countries,
chiefly in die tropics, where vast populations live capable of growing
economic needs which our manufacturers and merchants can sup-
ply. Our rivals are seizing and annexing territories for similar pur-
poses, and when they have annexed diem close diem to our trade.
The diplomacy and die arms of Great Britain must be used in order
to compel die owners of die new markets to deal widi us: and expe-
rience shows diat die safest means of securing and developing such
markets is by establishing 'protectorates' or by annexation. The pre-
sent value of these markets must not be taken as a final test of the
economy of such a policy; die process of educating civilised needs
which we can supply is of necessity a gradual one, and die cost of
such Imperialism must be regarded as a capital ouday, die fruits of
which posterity will reap. The new markets may not be large, but
diey form serviceable oudets for the overflow of our great textile and
metal industries, and, when die vast Asiatic and African populations
of die interior are reached, a rapid expansion of trade may be ex-
pected to result.

"Far larger and more important is die pressure of capital for ex-
ternal fields of investment. Moreover, while die manufacturer and
trader are well content to trade widi foreign nations, the tendency
for investors to work towards die political annexation of countries
which contain their more speculative investments is very powerful.
Of the fact of this pressure of capital diere can be no question.



IMPERIALISM



Large savings are made which cannot find any profitahle investment
in this country; they must find employment elsewhere, and it is to
the advantage of die nation that they should he employed as largely
as possible in lands where they can be utilised in opening up mar-
kets for British trade and employment for British enterprise.

"However costly, however perilous, this process of imperial ex-
pansion may be, it is necessary to the continued existence and pro-
gress of our nation; 21 if we abandoned it we must he content to leave
the development of the world to other nations, who will everywhere
cut into our trade, and even impair our means of securing the food
and raw materials we require to support our population. Imperial-
ism is thus seen to be, not a choice, hut a necessity."

The practical force of this economic argument in politics is strik-
ingly illustrated by the recent history of the United States. Here is a
country which suddenly breaks through a conservative policy,
strongly held by hotli political parties, hound up with every popular
instinct and tradition, and flings itself into a rapid imperial career for
which it possesses neither the material nor the moral equipment,
risking the principles and practices of liherty and equality by the es-
tablishment of militarism and the forcible subjugation of peoples
which it cannot safely admit to the condition of American citizen-
ship.

Is this a mere wild freak of spread-eaglism, a hurst of political
amhition on the part of a nation coming to a sudden realisation of its
destiny? Not at all. The spirit of adventure, the American "mission
of civilisation," are, as forces making for Imperialism, clearly subor-
dinate to the driving force of the economic factor. The dramatic
character of the change is due to the unprecedented rapidity of the
industrial revolution in the United States during the last two dec-
ades. During that period the United States, with her unrivalled natu-
ral resources, her immense resources of skilled and unskilled la-
hour, and her genius for invention and organisation, has developed
the best equipped and most productive manufacturing economy the
world has yet seen. Fostered by rigid protective tariffs, her metal,
textile, tool, clothing, furniture, and other manufactures have shot
up in a single generation from infancy to full maturity, and, having
passed through a period of intense competition, are attaining, under
the able control of great trust-makers, a power of production greater
than has been attained in the most advanced industrial countries of
Europe.

An era of cut-throat competition, followed by a rapid process of
amalgamation, has thrown an enormous quantity of wealth into the
hands of a small number of captains of industry. No luxury of living
to which this class could attain kept pace with its rise of income, and
a process of automatic saving set in upon an unprecedented scale.
The investment of these savings in other industries helped to bring
these under the same concentrative forces. Thus a great increase of



IMPERIALISM



savings seeking profitable investment is synchronous with a stricter
economy of the use of existing capital. No doubt the rapid growth of
a population, accustomed to a high and an always ascending stand-
ard of comfort, absorbs in the satisfaction of its wants a large quanti-
ty of new capital. But the actual rate of saving, conjoined with a
more economical application of forms of existing capital, has ex-
ceeded considerably the rise of the national consumption of manu-
factures. The power of production has far outstripped the actual rate
of consumption, and, contrary to the older economic theory, has
been unable to force a corresponding increase of consumption by
lowering prices.

This is no mere theory. The history of any of the numerous
trusts or combinations in the United States sets out the facts with
complete distinctness. In the free competition of manufactures pre-
ceding combination the chronic condition is one of "over-
production," in the sense that all the mills or factories can only be
kept at work by cutting prices down towards a point where the
weaker competitors are forced to close down, because they cannot
sell their goods at a price which covers the true cost of production.
The first result of the successful formation of a trust or combine is
to close down the worse equipped or worse placed mills, and supply
the entire market from the better equipped and better placed ones.
This course may or may not be attended by a rise of price and some
restriction of consumption: in some cases trusts take most of their
profits by raising prices, in other cases by reducing the costs of pro-
duction through employing only the best mills and stopping the
waste of competition.

For the present argument it matters not which course is taken;
the point is that this concentration of industry in "trusts," "com-
bines," &c, at once limits the quantity of capital which can be effec-
tively employed and increases the share of profits out of which fresh
savings and fresh capital will spring. It is quite evident that a trust
which is motived by cut-throat competition, due to an excess of capi-
tal, cannot normally find inside the "trusted" industry employment
for that portion of the profits which the trust-makers desire to save
and to invest. New inventions and other economies of production or
distribution within the trade may absorb some of the new capital,
but there are rigid limits to this absorption. The trust-maker in oil or
sugar must find other investments for his savings: if he is early in the
application of the combination principles to his trade, he will natu-
rally apply his surplus capital to establish similar combinations in
other industries, economising capital still further, and rendering it
ever harder for ordinary saving men to find investments for their
savings.

Indeed, the conditions alike of cut-throat competition and of
combination attest the congestion of capital in the manufacturing
industries which have entered the machine economy. We are not



IMPERIALISM



here concerned with any theoretic question as to tire possihility of
producing hy modern machine methods more goods than can find a
market. It is sufficient to point out that die manufacturing power of a
country like the United States may grow so fast as to exceed tire de-
mands of die home market. No one acquainted widi trade will deny
a fact which all American economists assert, diat diis is the condition
which the United States has reached within die last few years, far as
the more developed industries are concerned. Her manufactures
are saturated widi capital and can ahsorb no more. One after anoth-
er diey are seeking refuge from die waste of competition in "com-
bines" which secure a measure of profitable peace by restricting die
quantity of operative capital. Industrial and financial princes in oil,
steel, sugar, railroads, banking, &c, are faced with the dilemma of
eidier spending more dian they know how to spend, or forcing mar-
kets outside die home area. Two economic courses are open to
diem, bodi leading towards an abandonment of die political isola-
tion of the past and the adoption of imperialist mediods in the fu-
ture. Instead of shutting down inferior mills and rigidly restricting
output to correspond widi profitable sales in die home markets, diey
may employ dieir full productive power, applying dieir savings to
increase their business capital, and, while still regulating output and
prices for die home market, may "hustie" for foreign markets,
dumping down dieir surplus goods at prices which would not be
possible save for die profitable nature of dieir home market. So
likewise diey may employ dieir savings in seeking investments out-
side their country, first repaying die capital borrowed from Great
Britain and odier countries for die early development of dieir rail-
roads, mines and manufactures, and afterwards becoming them-
selves a creditor class to foreign countries.

It is diis sudden demand for foreign markets for manufactures
and for investments which is avowedly responsible for die adoption
of Imperialism as a political policy and practice by die Republican
party to which die great industrial and financial chiefs belong, and
which belongs to diem. The adventurous endiusiasm of President
Roosevelt and his "manifest destiny" and "mission of civilisation"
party must not deceive us. It is Messrs. Rockefeller, Pierpont Mor-
gan, Hanna, Schwab, and dieir associates who need Imperialism and
who are fastening it upon die shoulders of the great Republic of the
West. They need Imperialism because diey desire to use die public-
resources of their country to find profitable employment for die cap-
ital which odierwise would be superfluous.

It is not indeed necessary to own a country in order to do trade
widi it or to invest capital in it, and doubdess die United States can
find some vent for their surplus goods and capital in European
countries. But diese countries are for die most part able to make
provision for themselves; most of diem have erected tariffs against
manufacturing imports, and even Great Britain is being urged to de-



IMPERIALISM



fend herself by reverting to Protection. The big American manufac-
turers and financiers will be compelled to look to China and the Pa-
cific and to South America for their most profitable chances; Protec-
tionists by principle and practice, they will insist upon getting as
close a monopoly of these markets as they can secure, and the com-
petition of Germany, England, and other trading nations will drive
them to the establishment of special political relations with tire mar-
kets they most prize. Cuba, tire Philippines, and Hawaii are but the
hors d'oeuwe to whet air appetite for an ampler banquet. Moreover,
tire powerful hold upon politics which these industrial and financial
magnates possess forms a separate stimulus, which, as we have
shown, is operative in Great Britain and elsewhere; tire public ex-
penditure in pursuit of an imperial career will be a separate im-
mense source of profit to these men, as financiers negotiating loans,
shipbuilders and owners handling subsidies, contractors and manu-
facturers of armaments and other imperialist appliances.

The suddenness of this political revolution is due to tire rapid
manifestation of tire need. During a period of ten years tire United
States has nearly trebled tire value of its manufacturing export trade,
and, if the rate of progress of tire last few years continues, within this
decade it will overtake our more slowly advancing export trade, and
stand first in tire list of manufacture -exporting nations. 22

This is tire avowed ambition, and no idle one, of tire keenest
business men of America; and with tire natural resources, tire labour
and tire administrative talents at their disposal, it is quite likely they
will achieve their object. 21 The stronger and more direct control over
politics exercised in America by business men enables them to drive
more quickly and more straightly along tire line of their economic
interests than in Great Britain. American Imperialism is the natural
product of tire economic pressure of a sudden advance of capitalism
which cannot find occupation at home and needs foreign markets
for goods and for investments.

The same needs exist in European countries, and, as is admit-
ted, drive Governments along tire same path. Over-production in
tire sense of air excessive manufacturing plant, and surplus capital
which cannot find sound investments within tire country, force Great
Britain, Germany, Holland, France to place larger and larger por-
tions of their economic resources outside tire area of their present
political domain, and then stimulate a policy of political expansion
so as to take in tire new areas. The economic sources of this move-
ment are laid bare by periodic trade -depressions due to an inability
of producers to find adequate and profitable markets for what they
can produce. The Majority Report of tire Commission upon the
Depression of Trade in f 885 put tire matter in a nut-shell. "That,
owing to tire nature of tire times, tire demand for our commodities
does not increase at the same rate as formerly; that our capacity for
production is consequently in excess of our requirements, and could



IMPERIALISM



be considerably increased at short notice; diat diis is due pardy to
die competition of the capital which is being steadily accumulated in
die country." The Minority Report straightiy imputes die condition
of affairs to "over-production." Germany is at the present time suf-
fering severely from what is called a glut of capital and of manufac-
turing power: she must have new markets; her Consuls all over die
world are "hustling" for trade; trading setdements are forced upon
Asia Minor; in East and West Africa, in China and elsewhere the
German Empire is impelled to a policy of colonisation and protec-
torates as outiets for German Commercial energy.

Every improvement of methods of production, every concentra-
tion of ownership and control, seems to accentuate die tendency. As
one nation after anodier enters die machine economy and adopts
advanced industrial mediods, it becomes more difficult for its manu-
facturers, merchants, and financiers to dispose profitably of their
economic resources, and diey are tempted more and more to use
dieir Governments in order to secure for their particular use some
distant undeveloped country by annexation and protection.

The process we may be told is inevitable, and so it seems upon a
superficial inspection. Everywhere appear excessive powers of pro-
duction, excessive capital in search of investment. It is admitted by
all business men diat die growth of die powers of production in dieir
country exceeds the growth in consumption, that more goods can be
produced dian can be sold at a profit, and diat more capital exists
dian can find remunerative investment.

It is diis economic condition of affairs diat forms die taproot of
Imperialism. If die consuming public in diis country raised its stand-
ard of consumption to keep pace with every rise of productive pow-
ers, diere could be no excess of goods or capital clamorous to use
Imperialism in order to find markets: foreign trade would indeed
exist, but diere would be no difficulty in exchanging a small surplus
of our manufactures for the food and raw material we annually ab-
sorbed, and all die savings diat we made could find employment, if
we chose, in home industries.

There is nodiing inherentiy irrational in such a supposition.
Whatever is, or can be, produced, can be consumed, for a claim
upon it, as rent, profit, or wages, forms part of the real income of
some member of die community, and he can consume it, or else
exchange it for some odier consumable widi some one else who will
consume it. Widi everything diat is produced a consuming power is
born. If dien diere are goods which cannot get consumed, or which
cannot even get produced because it is evident diey cannot get con-
sumed, and if diere is a quantity of capital and labour which cannot
get full employment because its products cannot get consumed, die
only possible explanation of diis paradox is die refusal of owners of
consuming power to apply diat power in effective demand for
commodities.



IMPERIALISM



It is, of course, possible that an excess of producing power might
exist in particular industries by misdirection, being engaged in cer-
tain manufactures, whereas it ought to have been engaged in agricul-
ture or some other use. But no one can seriously contend that such
misdirection explains die recurrent gluts and consequent depres-
sions of modern industry, or diat, when over-production is manifest
in die leading manufactures, ample avenues are open for die surplus
capital and labour in odier industries. The general character of the
excess of producing power is proved by die existence at such times
of large bank stocks of idle money seeking any sort of profitable in-
vestment and finding none.

The root questions underlying die phenomena are clearly these:
"Why is it diat consumption fails to keep pace automatically in a
community widi power of production?" "Why does under-con-
sumption or over-saving occur?" For it is evident that die consuming
power, which, if exercised, would keep tense die reins of produc-
tion, is in part withheld, or in other words is "saved" and stored up
for investment. All saving for investment does not imply slackness of
production; quite die contrary. Saving is economically justified, from
die social standpoint, when die capital in which it takes material
shape finds full employment in helping to produce commodities
which, when produced, will be consumed. It is saving in excess of
this amount that causes mischief, taking shape in surplus capital
which is not needed to assist current consumption, and which either
lies idle, or tries to oust existing capital from its employment, or else
seeks speculative use abroad under the protection of the Govern-
ment.

But it may be asked, "Why should there be any tendency to
over-saving? why should die owners of consuming power withhold a
larger quantity for savings than can be serviceably employed?" An-
other way of putting die same question is this, "Why should not die
pressure of present wants keep pace with every possibility of satisfy-
ing diem?" The answer to diese pertinent questions carries us to the
broadest issue of die distribution of wealth. If a tendency to dis-
tribute income or consuming power according to needs were opera-
tive, it is evident diat consumption would rise widi ever}' rise of pro-
ducing power, for human needs are illimitable, and diere could be
no excess of saving. But it is quite otherwise in a state of economic
society where distribution has no fixed relation to needs, but is de-
termined by odier conditions which assign to some people a con-
suming power vastly in excess of needs or possible uses, while others
are destitute of consuming power enough to satisfy even the full de-
mands of physical efficiency. The following illustration may serve to
make the issue clear.

"The volume of production has been constantly rising owing to the develop-
ment of modern machinery. There are two main channels to carry off diese



IMPERIALISM



products— one channel carrying off the product destined to be consumed by the
workers, and the other channel carrying off die remainder to the rich. The work-
ers' channel is in rock-bound banks that cannot enlarge, owing to the competitive
wage system preventing wages rising pro rata with increased efficiency. Wages are
based upon cost of living, and not upon efficiency of labour. The miner in the
poor mine gets the same wages per day as the miner in the adjoining rich mine.
The owner of the rich mine gets the advantage— not his labourer. The channel
which conveys die goods destined to supply the rich is itself divided into two
streams. One stream carries off what the rich 'spend' on themselves for die neces-
sities and luxuries of life. The other is simply an 'overflow' stream carrying off
their 'savings,' The channel for spending, i.e. the amount wasted by the rich in
luxuries, may broaden somewhat, but owing to the small number of diose rich
enough to indulge in whims it can never be greatly enlarged, and at any rate it
bears such a small proportion to the other channel that in no event can much
hope of avoiding a flood of capital be hoped for from tliis division. The rich will
never be so ingenious as to spend enough to prevent overproduction. The great
safety overflow channel which has been continuously more and more widened
and deepened to carry off the ever-increasing flood of new capital is that division
of the stream which carried the savings of the rich, and this is not only suddenly
found to be incapable of further enlargement, but actually seems to be in the pro-
cess of being dammed up." 2 '

Though this presentation over-accentuates the cleavage hetween
rich and poor and over-states the weakness of the workers, it gives
forcible and sound expression to a most important and ill-
recognised economic truth. The "overflow" stream of savings is of
course fed not exclusively from the surplus income of "the rich"; the
professional and industrial middle classes, and to some slight extent
the workers, contribute. But the "flooding" is distinctly due to the
automatic saving of the surplus income of rich men. This is of
course particularly true of America, where multi-millionaires rise
quickly and find themselves in possession of incomes far exceeding
the demands of any craving that is known to them. To make the
metaphor complete, the overflow stream must he represented as re-
entering the stream of production and seeking to empty there all the
"savings" that it carries. Where competition remains free, the result
is a chronic congestion of productive power and of production, forc-
ing down home prices, wasting large sums in advertising and in
pushing for orders, and periodically causing a crisis followed by a
collapse, during which quantities of capital and labour lie unem-
ployed and unremunerated. The prime object of the trust or other
combine is to remedy this waste and loss by substituting regulation
of output for reckless overproduction. In achieving this it actually
narrows or even dams up the old channels of investment, limiting
the overflow stream to the exact amount required to maintain the
normal current of output. But this rigid limitation of trade, though
required for the separate economy of each trust, does not suit the
trust-maker, who is driven to compensate for strictly regulated in-
dustry at home by cutting new foreign channels as outlets for his
productive power and his excessive savings. Thus we reach the con-



IMPERIALISM



elusion that Imperialism is the endeavour of tire great controllers of
industry to broaden die channel for dre flow of dieir surplus wealdr
by seeking foreign markets and foreign investments to take off dre
goods and capital diey cannot sell or use at home.

The fallacy of dre supposed inevitability of imperial expansion as
a necessary oudet for progressive industry is now manifest. It is not
industrial progress that demands dre opening up of new markets and
areas of investment, but mal-distribution of consuming power which
prevents die absorption of commodities and capital within die coun-
try. The over-saving which is die economic root of Imperialism is
found by analysis to consist of rents, monopoly profits, and other
unearned or excessive elements of income, which, not being earned
by labour of head or hand, have no legitimate raison d'etre. Having
no natural relation to effort of production, diey impel dieir recipi-
ents to no corresponding satisfaction of consumption: diey form a
surplus wealth, which, having no proper place in the normal econ-
omy of production and consumption, tends to accumulate as exces-
sive savings. Let any turn in die tide of politico-economic forces di-
vert from diese owners dieir excess of income and make it flow, ei-
tlier to die workers in higher wages, or to the community in taxes, so
that it will be spent instead of being saved, serving in eidier of these
ways to swell the tide of consumption— there will be no need to fight
for foreign markets or foreign areas of investment.

Many have carried dieir analysis so far as to realise die absurdity
of spending half our financial resources in fighting to secure foreign
markets at times when hungry moudis, ill-clad backs, ill-furnished
houses indicate coundess unsatisfied material wants among our own
population. If we may take the careful statistics of Mr. Rowntree 25 for
our guide, we shall be aware that more dian one-fourdi of the popu-
lation of our towns is living at a standard which is below bare physi-
cal efficiency. If, by some economic readjustment, die products
which flow from die surplus saving of die rich to swell die overflow
streams could be diverted so as to raise the incomes and die stand-
ard of consumption of this inefficient fourdi, there would be no
need for pushful Imperialism, and die cause of social reform would
have won its greatest victory.

It is not inherent in the nature of tilings that we should spend
our natural resources on militarism, war, and risky, unscrupulous
diplomacy, in order to find markets for our goods and surplus capi-
tal. An intelligent progressive community, based upon substantial
equality of economic and educational opportunities, will raise its
standard of consumption to correspond with every increased power
of production, and can find full employment for an unlimited quan-
tity of capital and labour within the limits of die country which it oc-
cupies. Where die distribution of incomes is such as to enable all
classes of the nation to convert their felt wants into an effective de-
mand for commodities, diere can be no over-production, no under-



IMPERIALISM



employment of capital and labour, and no necessity to light for for-
eign markets.

The most convincing condemnation of the current economy is
conveyed in the difficulty which producers everywhere experience in
finding consumers for their products: a fact attested by the prodi-
gious growth of classes of agents and middlemen, the multiplication
of every sort of advertising, and the general increase of the distribu-
tive classes. Under a sound economy the pressure would be re-
versed: die growing wants of progressive societies would be a con-
stant stimulus to the inventive and operative energies of producers,
and would form a constant strain upon the powers of production.
The simultaneous excess of all the factors of production, attested by
frequently recurring periods of trade depression, is a most dramatic
exhibition of the false economy of distribution. It does not imply a
mere miscalculation in the application of productive power, or a
brief temporary excess of that power; it manifests in an acute form
an economic waste which is chronic and general throughout the ad-
vanced industrial nations, a waste contained in the divorcement of
the desire to consume and the power to consume.

If the apportionment of income were such as to evoke no exces-
sive saving, full constant employment for capital and labour would
be furnished at home. This, of course, does not imply that there
would be no foreign trade. Goods that could not be produced at
home, or produced as well or as cheaply, would still be purchased
by ordinary process of international exchange, but here again the
pressure would be the wholesome pressure of the consumer anxious
to buy abroad what he could not buy at home, not the blind eager-
ness of die producer to use every force or trick of trade or politics to
find markets for his "surplus" goods.

The struggle for markets, the greater eagerness of producers to
sell than of consumers to buy, is die crowning proof of a false econ-
omy of distribution. Imperialism is die fruit of this false economy;
"social reform" is its remedy. The primary purpose of "social re-
form," using die term in its economic signification, is to raise die
wholesome standard of private and public consumption for a nation,
so as to enable die nation to live up to its highest standard of pro-
duction. Even those social reformers who aim directly at abolishing
or reducing some bad form of consumption, as in the Temperance
movement, generally recognise die necessity of substituting some
better form of current consumption which is more educative and
stimulative of other tastes, and will assist to raise the general stand-
ard of consumption. There is no necessity to open up new foreign
markets; the home markets are capable of indefinite expansion.
Whatever is produced in England can be consumed in England,
provided that die "income," or power to demand commodities, is
properly distributed. This only appears untrue because of die un-
natural and unwholesome specialisation to which this country has



IMPERIALISM



been subjected, based upon a bad distribution of economic re-
sources, which has induced an overgrowth of certain manufacturing
trades for the express purpose of effecting foreign sales. If the indus-
trial revolution had taken place in an England founded upon equal
access by all classes to land, education and legislation, specialisation
in manufactures would not have gone so far (though more intelligent
progress would have been made, by reason of a widening of the area
of selection of inventive and organising talents); foreign trade would
have been less important, though more steady; tire standard of life
for all portions of the population would have been high, and the
present rate of national consumption would probably have given
full, constant, remunerative employment to a far larger quantity of
private and public capital than is now employed. 26 For the over-
saving or wider consumption that is traced to excessive incomes of
the rich is a suicidal economy, even from tire exclusive standpoint of
capital; for consumption alone vitalises capital and makes it capable
of yielding profits. Air economy that assigns to tire "possessing" clas-
ses an excess of consuming power which they cannot use, and can-
not convert into really serviceable capital, is a dog-in-the -manger pol-
icy. The social reforms which deprive tire possessing classes of their
surplus will not, therefore, inflict upon them tire real injury they
dread; they can only use this surplus by forcing on their country a
wrecking policy of Imperialism. The only safety of nations lies in
removing tire unearned increments of income from tire possessing
classes, and adding them to tire wage-income of the working classes
or to the public income, in order that they may be spent in raising
the standard of consumption.

Social reform bifurcates, according as reformers seek to achieve
this end by raising wages or by increasing public taxation and ex-
penditure. These courses are not essentially contradictory, but are
rather complementary. Working-class movements aim, either by
private cooperation or by political pressure on legislative and admin-
istrative government, at increasing tire proportion of tire national
income which accrues to labour in tire form of wages, pensions,
compensation for injuries, &c. State Socialism aims at getting for the
direct use of tire whole society an increased share of tire "social val-
ues" which arise from tire closely and essentially co-operative work
of an industrial society, taxing property and incomes so as to draw
into the public exchequer for public expenditure tire "unearned el-
ements" of income, leaving to individual producers those incomes
which are necessary to induce them to apply in tire best way their
economic energies, and to private enterprises those businesses
which do not breed monopoly, and which tire public need not or
cannot undertake. These are not, indeed, the sole or perhaps tire
best avowed objects of social reform movements. But for tire pur-
poses of this analysis they form tire kernel.

Trade Unionism and Socialism are thus tire natural enemies of



IMPERIALISM



Imperialism, for they take away from die "imperialist" classes the
surplus incomes which form the economic stimulus of Imperialism.

This does not pretend to he a final statement of die full relations
of diese forces. When we come to political analysis we shall per-
ceive diat die tendency of Imperialism is to crush Trade Unionism
and to "nihhle" at or parasitically exploit State Socialism. But, con-
fining ourselves for die present to die narrowly economic setting,
Trade Unionism and State Socialism may he regarded as com-
plementary forces arrayed against Imperialism, in as far as, hy divert-
ing to working-class or puhlic expenditure elements of income
which would otherwise he surplus savings, diey raise the general
standard of home consumption and ahate die pressure for foreign
markets. Of course, if die increase of working-class income were
wholly or chiefly "saved," not spent, or if die taxation of unearned
incomes were utilised for die relief of other taxes home hy die pos-
sessing classes, no such result as we have descrihed would follow.
There is, however, no reason to anticipate diis result from trade-
union or socialistic measures. Though no sufficient natural stimulus
exists to force the well-to-do classes to spend in furdier luxuries die
surplus incomes which diey save, every working-class family is sub-
ject to powerful stimuli of economic needs, and a reasonably gov-
erned State would regard as its prime duty die relief of die present
poverty of public life by new forms of socially useful expenditure.

But we are not here concerned widi what belongs to the practical
issues of political and economic policy. It is the economic theory for
which we claim acceptance— a dieoiy which, if accurate, dispels the
delusion that expansion of foreign trade, and dierefore of empire, is
a necessity of national life.

Regarded from the standpoint of economy of energy, die same
"choice of life" confronts die nation as die individual. An individual
may expend all his energy in acquiring external possessions, adding
field to field, barn to barn, factory to factory— may "spread himself
over die widest area of property, amassing material wealdi which is
in some sense "himself as containing die impress of his power and
interest. He does this by specialising upon the lower acquisitive
plane of interest at die cost of neglecting die cultivation of die higher
qualities and interests of his nature. The antagonism is not indeed
absolute. Aristotle has said, "We must first secure a livelihood and
dien practise virtue." Hence die pursuit of material property as a
reasonable basis of physical comfort would be held true economy by
the wisest men; but die absorption of time, energy, and interest up-
on such quantitative expansion at die necessary cost of starving the
higher tastes and faculties is condemned as false economy. The
same issue conies up in die business life of the individual: it is die
question of intensive versus extensive cultivation. A rude or ignorant
farmer, where land is plentiful, is apt to spread his capital and labour
over a large area, taking in new tracts and cultivating diem poorly. A



IMPERIALISM



skilled, scientific farmer will study a smaller patch of land, cultivate it
thoroughly, and utilise its diverse properties, adapting it to the spe-
cial needs of his most remunerative markets. The same is true of
other husinesses; even where tire economy of large-scale production
is greatest there exists some limit heyond which the wise husiness
man will not go, aware that in doing so he will risk by enfeebled
management what he seems to gain by mechanical economies of
production and market.

Everywhere tire issue of quantitative versus qualitative growth
comes up. This is tire entire issue of empire. A people limited in
number and energy and in the land they occupy have the choice of
improving to the utmost the political and economic management of
their own land, confining themselves to such accessions of territory
as are justified by tire most economical disposition of a growing
population; or they may proceed, like tire slovenly farmer, to spread
their power and energy over the whole earth, tempted by the specu-
lative value or the quick profits of some new market, or else by mere
greed of territorial acquisition, and ignoring the political and eco-
nomic wastes and risks involved by this imperial career. It must be
clearly understood that this is essentially a choice of alternatives; a
full simultaneous application of intensive and extensive cultivation is
impossible. A nation may either, following tire example of Denmark
or Switzerland, put brains into agriculture, develop a finely varied
system of public education, general and technical, apply the ripest
science to its special manufacturing industries, and so support in
progressive comfort and character a considerable population upon a
strictly limited area; or it may, like Great Britain, neglect its agricul-
ture, allowing its lands to go out of cultivation and its population to
grow up in towns, fall behind other nations in its methods of educa-
tion and in tire capacity of adapting to its uses the latest scientific
knowledge, in order that it may squander its pecuniary and military
resources in forcing bad markets and finding speculative fields of
investment in distant comers of the earth, adding millions of square
miles and of unassimilable population to the area of tire Empire.

The driving forces of class interest which stimulate and support
this false economy we have explained. No remedy will serve which
permits tire future operation of these forces. It is idle to attack Im-
perialism or Militarism as political expedients or policies unless the
axe is laid at the economic root of the tree, and tire classes for whose
interest Imperialism works are shorn of tire surplus revenues which
seek this outlet.



IMPERIALISM



CHAPTER VII

IMPERIALIST FINANCE

THE analysis of economic forces in die foregoing chapter explains
the character which puhlic finance assumes in States committed to
an imperialist policy. Imperialism, as we see, implies the use of die
machinery of government hy private interests, mainly capitalist, to
secure for diem economic gains outside dieir country. The domi-
nance of diis factor in puhlic policy imposes a special character alike
upon expenditure and taxation.

The accompanying diagram [missing from the original text; de-
rived from British Budgets (Mallet & George)] brings into clear light
die main features of die national expenditure of Great Britain dur-
ing die last diree decades of die nineteenth century.

The first feature is die rate of growdi of national expenditure
taken as a whole. This growth has been far faster dian die growth of
foreign trade. Eor whereas die average yearly value of our foreign
trade for 1870-75, amounting to £636,000,000, increased in die pe-
riod 1895-98 to £737,000,000, the average public expenditure ad-
vanced over the same period from £63,160,000 to £94,450,000. It is
faster than die growdi of die aggregate national income, which, ac-
cording to die rough estimates of statisticians, advanced during die
same period from about £1,200,000,000 to £1,700,000,000. The
rate of growdi has gready quickened during die later half of die pe-
riod in question, for, leaving out of consideration war expenditure,
die rise of ordinary imperial expenditure has been from.
£87,423,000 in 1888 to £128,600,000 in 1900.

The most salient feature of die diagram is die small proportion
of the national revenue expended for what may be regarded as di-
rectiy productive purposes of government. Roughly speaking, diree-
quarters of die money goes for naval and military expenditure, and
for die payment of military debts, about five shillings in die pound
being available for education, civil government, and die dubious pol-
icy of grants in aid of local taxation. 27

The only satisfactory incident disclosed by die table is die grow-
ing amount and proportion of public money spent on education. A
substantial part of die sum expended as aid to local taxation has
simply gone as a dole to landowners.

The direct military and naval expenditure during die period has
increased faster dian die total expenditure, the growdi of trade, of
national income, or any odier general indication of national re-
sources. In 1875 die army and navy cost less dian 24} millions out of
a total expenditure of 65 millions; in 1898 diey cost nearly 43 mil-
lions out of a total of 99 millions.

The enormous expenditure upon the South African war will
admittedly be followed by a demand for a large permanent increase



IMPERIALISM



in these branches of expenditure, amounting to an addition of not
less than £15,000,000 per annum.

This growth of naval and military expenditure from about 25 to
60 millions in a little over a quarter of a century is the most signifi-
cant fact of imperialist finance. The financial, industrial, and profes-
sional classes, who, we have shown, form the economic core of Im-
perialism, have used their political power to extract these sums from
the nation in order to improve their investments and open up new
fields for capital, and to find profitable markets for their surplus
goods, while out of the public sums expended on these objects they
reap other great private gains in the shape of profitable contracts,
and lucrative or honourable employment.

The financial and industrial capitalists who have mainly engi-
neered this policy, employing their own genuine convictions to con-
ceal their ill-recognised business ends, have also made important
bribes or concessions to other less directly benefited interests in or-
der to keep their sympathy and ensure their support.

This explains the large and growing grants in aid of local taxa-
tion, almost the whole of which, interpreted by a scientific regard to
incidence of taxation, must be considered as a subsidy to landown-
ers. The support of the Church and of the liquor trade has been
more cheaply purchased; the former by relief of rates on tithes and
increased grants for Church schools, the latter by a policy of master-
ly inaction in the matter of temperance reforms and special consid-
eration in regard to taxation.

In making tire capitalist-imperialist forces the pivot of financial
policy, I do not mean that other forces, industrial and political, have
no independent aims and influences, but simply that the former
group must be regarded as tire true determinant in tire interpretation
of actual policy.

We have identified almost all tire organised interests, commonly
summed under tire head of Capitalism, including laird capital, with
Imperialism. Most of them participate directly in one or other of the
two sorts of gain which attend this policy: the interest, trade profits,
or employment furnished by the imperialist policy, or the interest,
profit, or employment connected with military and civil expenditure
itself.

It cannot be too clearly recognised that increasing public ex-
penditure, apart from all political justification, is a direct source of
gain to certain well-organised and influential interests, and to all such
Imperialism is the chief instrument of such increasing expenditure.

While the directors of this definitely parasitic policy are capital-
ists, the same motives appeal to special classes of tire workers. In
many towns most important trades are dependent upon Govern-
ment employment or contracts; tire Imperialism of tire metal and
shipbuilding centres is attributable in no small degree to this fact.
Members of Parliament freely employ their influence to secure con-



IMPERIALISM



tracts and direct trade to their constituents, and every growth of pub-
lic expenditure enhances diis dangerous bias.

The clearest significance of imperialist finance, however, ap-
pears on die side, not of expenditure, but of taxation. The object of
diose economic interests which use die public purse for purposes of
private gain is in large measure defeated if diey have first to find die
money to fill diat purse. To avert die direct incidence of taxation
from dieir own shoulders on to those of odier classes or of posterity
is a natural policy of self-defence.

A sane policy of taxation would derive die whole or die main
part of die national revenue from unearned increments of land val-
ues and from profits in trades which, by virtue of some legal or eco-
nomic protection screening diem from close competition, are able
to earn high rates of interest or profit. Such taxation would be borne
most easily, falling upon unearned elements of incomes, and would
cause no disturbance of industry. This, however, would imply the
taxation of precisely diose elements which constitute die economic
taproot of Imperialism. For it is precisely die unearned elements of
income which tend towards an automatic process of accumulation,
and which, by swelling die stream of surplus capital seeking markets
of investment or markets for die surplus goods it helps to make, di-
rect political forces into Imperialism. A sound system of taxation
would, dierefore, strike at die very root of die malady.

On the other hand, were die capitalist-imperialist forces openly
to shift die burden of taxation on to die shoulders of the people, it
would be difficult under popular forms of government to operate
such an expensive policy. The people must pay, but diey must not
know diey are paying, or how much diey are paying, and the pay-
ment must be spread over as long a period as possible.

To take a concrete example from recent history. The medley of
financial and political interests which inveigled Great Britain into
spending some two hundred millions of public money, in order to
obtain for diem control of die land and mineral resources of the
Soudi African Republics, could not possibly have achieved dieir ob-
ject if diey had been compelled to raise the money by sending round
a tax-gadierer to take from ever}' citizen in hard cash die several
pounds which constituted his share of die taxes— die share which by
more crooked ways was to be got out of him.

To support Imperialism by direct taxation of incomes or proper-
ty would be impossible. Where any real forms of popular control
existed, militarism and wars would be impossible if every citizen was
made to realise dieir cost by payments of hard cash. Imperialism,
dierefore, makes everywhere for indirect taxation; not chiefly on
grounds of convenience, but for purposes of concealment. Or per-
haps it would be more just to say diat Imperialism takes advantage
of die cowardly and foolish preference which die average man eve-
rywhere exhibits for being tricked out of his contribution to die pub-



IMPERIALISM



lie funds, using this common folly for its own purposes. It is seldom
possible for any Government, even in the stress of some grave
emergency, to impose an income-tax; even a property-tax is com-
monly evaded in all cases of personal property, and is always un-
popular. The case of England is an exception which really proves
die rule.

The repeal of import duties and the establishment of Eree
Trade marked the political triumph of the new manufacturing and
commercial plutocracy over the landowning aristocracy. Free Trade
was so profitable to the former classes in securing cheap importation
of raw materials and in cheapening the subsistence of labour at a
time when England's priority in new industrial methods offered an
indefinitely rapid expansion of trade that they were willing to sup-
port the reimposition of the income-tax which Peel proposed in
1842 in order to enable him to repeal or reduce the import duties.
When the sudden financial stress of the Crimean war came on the
country the Free Trade policy was in the prime of its popularity and
success, and a Liberal ministry, in preference to a reversion to Pro-
tection which would otherwise have been inevitable, gave perma-
nency to the tax, extending the area of its application and making its
removal more difficult by further repeals of import duties. No Gov-
ernment could now remove it, for the new unpopularity caused by
finding adequate substitutes would have outweighed the credit
gained by its removal, while its productivity and calculability are ad-
vantages shared in an equal degree by no other mode of taxation.

Some allowance may also be made for the principles and per-
sonal convictions of political financiers trained in the English science
of political economy, and still more for the temptation of competing
parties to seek the favour of the newly enfranchised populace by a
well-paraded policy of class taxation. The seedling revolutionism of
die mid-century throughout Europe, die rapid growdi of huge indus-
trial centres throughout England, with their masses of ill-explored
poverty and their known aptitude for ignorant agitation, made the
