within and cannot be imposed by alien instruction from without.

That die squabbles of European potentates for territorial expan-
sion, the lusts of merchants or financiers, die ludicrously false expec-
tations of missionaries, die catch-words of political parties in Euro-
pean elections, should be driving European nations to destroy the
civilisation of a quarter of die human race without possessing the
ability or even recognising die need to provide a substitute, ought
surely to give pause to those Imperialists who claim to base their
policy on reason and the common good.

No thinking man can seriously question die immense im-
portance of free intercourse between die West and the East, or
doubt die gain diat would accrue to die civilisation of die world by a
wise communication to die Eastern mind of diose arts which peculi-
arly represent Western civilisation, die laborious, successful study of
the physical sciences and their application to the arts of industry, the
systematic development of certain definite principles and practices
of law and government, and die diought and literature which are die
conscious flowering of this growdi of practical achievement.

That Europe could in this way render an invaluable service to
Asia is certain.

"Some strange fiat of arrest, probably due to mental exhaustion,
has condemned die brown men and die yellow men to eternal re-
production of old ideas." 120 To revivify the mind of Asia, to set it
working again along new lines of rich productivity, this might be die
boon of Europe. And for diis sendee she too might take a rich re-
ward. The brooding mind of Asia gave to sluggish Europe in past
ages die great momenta in religion and philosophy and in die math-
ematics; even in its sleep, or what appears to us die sleep of many
centuries, it may have had its noble and illuminative dreams. The
reason of die West may yet need die insight of die East. An union
so profitable in the past may not be barren for the future. It is the
right condition of diis wholesome intercourse which is of supreme
importance to die cause of civilisation. Now one tiling at least is cer-
tain. Force and die pushful hand of material greed inhibit die free
interaction of mind and mind essential to diis intercourse. The an-
cient civilisations of India and China, whose duration bears testimo-



IMPERIALISM



ny to inherent qualities of worth, have not been directed chiefly to
die attainment of progress in die arts of material wealth, diough die
simpler industries have in parts of China and India attained a high
perfection, but radier to die maintenance of certain small types of
orderly social life, widi a strong hierarchy of social and industrial
ranks in India, with a fundamentally democratic character in China.

The energy spared from political and industrial struggles, and in
China from military practices, has gone, partly to die cultivation of
certain simple qualities of domestic life and personal conduct, pardy
to die wide diffusion of a certain real life of die soul, animated by
profound religious and philosophic speculations and contemplations
in India, or by die elaboration of a more practical, utilitarian wisdom
in China. These Eastern civilisations alone have stood the test of
time; the qualities which have enabled diem to survive ought surely
to be matter of deep concern for die mushroom civilisations of die
West. It may even be true that the maintenance of diese younger
and more unstable civilisations depends upon unlocking die treas-
ure-house of the wisdom of die East. Whedier this be so or not, die
violent breaking down of die characteristic institutions of Asia to sat-
isfy some hasty lust of commerce, or some greed of power, is quite
the most fatally blind misreading of die true process of world-
civilisation diat it is possible to conceive. Eor Europe to rule Asia by
force for purposes of gain,, and to justify that rule by die pretence
diat she is civilising Asia and raising her to a higher level of spiritual
life, will be adjudged by history, perhaps, to be the crowning wrong
and folly of Imperialism. What Asia has to give, her priceless stores
of wisdom garnered from her experience of ages, we refuse to take;
die much or littie which we could give we spoil by die brutal manner
of our giving. This is what Imperialism has done, and is doing, for
Asia.



CHAPTER VI

IMPERIAL FEDERATION
I

THE imperial policy of Great Britain since 1870, and more particu-
larly since 1885, has been almost entirely absorbed in promoting die
subjugation and annexation of tracts of territory where no genuine
white settlement of any magnitude is contemplated. This policy, as
we have seen, differs essentially from colonisation; and from the
standpoint of government it implies a progressive diminution of
freedom in die British Empire by constantly increasing die propor-
tion of its subjects who are destitute of real power of self-gov-
ernment.



IMPERIALISM



It is important to consider how this new Imperialism reacts, and
is likely in the future to react, upon the relations hetween Great
Britain and her self-governing colonies. Will it stimulate these colo-
nies to an assertion of growing independence and final formal sever-
ance from tire mother country, or will it lead them to form a closer
political union with her upon a hasis, no longer of Empire, hut of a
Federation of equal States? This is a vital issue, for it is quite certain
that the present relations will not he maintained.

Hitherto the tendency has been towards a steady consistent in-
crease of self-government, and a growing relaxation of Empire in tire
shape of control exercised by tire home Government. In Australasia,
North America, and South Africa seventeen self-governing colonies
have been established, endowed with reduced types of tire British
constitution. In tire case of Australia and of Canada tire growth of
self-government has been formally and actually advanced by acts of
federation, which have, in fact, especially in Australia, compensated
the restriction of tire power of tire federated States by a more than
equivalent increase of governing power vested in tire federal Gov-
ernment.

Great Britain has in tire main learned well tire lesson of the
American Revolution; she has not only permitted but favoured this
growing independence of her Australian and American colonies.
During tire very period when she has been occupied in the con-
scious policy of extending her Empire over lands which she cannot
colonise and must hold by force, she has been loosening her "im-
perial" hold over her white colonies. While 1873 removed tire last
bond of economic control which marked tire old "plantation" poli-
cy, by repealing tire Act of 1850 which had forbidden Australian
colonies from imposing differential duties as between tire colonies
and foreign countries, and permitting them in future to tax one an-
other's goods, the Australian Commonwealth Act of 1900 has, by
the powers accorded to its Federal Judicature, reduced to the nar-
rowest limits yet attained tire constitutional control of tire Privy
Council, and has by tire powers enabling tire Federal Government to
raise a central armed force for defence obtained a new substantial
basis for a possible national independence in tire future. Though it
is unlikely for some time to come that tire federal Government
which is contemplated for British South Africa will be accorded
powers equivalent to those of tire Australian or even tire Canadian
Federations, tire same tendency to increased self-government has in
the past steadily prevailed in Cape Colony and Natal, and it is toler-
ably certain that, if tire racial animosities between tire two white races
are abated, a South African Commonwealth would soon be found in
possession of a far larger measure of real self-government than tire
British colonies which enter it have hitherto possessed.

But while tire trend of British colonialism has uniformly been
towards increased self-government or practical independence, and



IMPERIALISM



has been appreciably strengthened by die process of federating co-
lonial States, it is evident diat die imperial statesmen who have fa-
voured most diis federation policy have had in view some larger re-
casting of the political relations with die mother country, which
should bind parent and children in closer family bonds, not merely
of affection or of trading intercourse, but of political association.
Though imperial federation for British purposes is no modern in-
vention, Lord Carnarvon was die first Colonial Secretary to set it
before him as a distinct object of attainment, favouring federation in
die various groups of colonies as the first step in a process which
should federate die Empire. The successful completion in 1873 of
the process of federation which formed die Dominion of Canada
doubdess stimulated Lord Carnarvon, entering office the next year,
to furdier experiments along similar lines. Unfortunately he laid
hands upon Soudi Africa for his forcing process, and suffered a dis-
astrous failure. Twenty years later Mr. Chamberlain resumed the
task, and, confronted by die same essential difficulties, die forcible
annexation of die two Dutch Republics, and die coercion of Cape
Colony, has brought his federation policy in South Africa towards
completion, while die federation of Australian States marks another
and a safer triumph of die federation principle.

The process of federation, as bearing on the relations of die fed-
erating colonies, is of course a triumph for die centripetal forces;
but, by securing a larger measure of dieoretical and practical inde-
pendence for die federal Governments, it has been centrifugal from
die standpoint of die Imperial Government. The work of securing
an effective political imperial federation implies, dierefore, a rever-
sal of hidierto dominant tendencies.

It is quite evident diat a strong and increasing desire for imperial
federation has been growing among a large number of British politi-
cians. So far as Mr. Chamberlain and some of his friends are con-
cerned, it dates back to die beginning of die struggle over Mr. Glad-
stone's Home Rule for Ireland policy. Speaking on Mr. Gladstone's
Home Rule Bill in 1886, Mr. Chamberlain said: "I should look for
die solution in die direction of die principle of federation. My right
honourable friend has looked for his model to die relations between
diis country and her self-governing and practically independent col-
onies. I diink diat is of doubtful expediency. The present connec-
tion between our colonies and ourselves is no doubt very strong,
owing to die affection which exists between members of die same
nation. But it is a sentimental tie, and a sentimental tie only. ... It
appears to me that the advantage of a system of federation is diat
Ireland might under it really remain an integral part of die Empire.
The action of such a scheme is centripetal and not centrifugal, and it
is in die direction of federation diat die democratic movement has
made most advances in die present century."

Now, it is quite true that the democratic movement, both now



IMPERIALISM



and in the future, seems closely linked with die formation of federal
States, and the federation of the parts of the British Empire appears
to suggest, as a next step and logical outcome, the federation of the
whole.

Holding, as we must, that any reasonable security for good order
and civilisation in the world implies the growing application of the
federation principle in international politics, it will appear only natu-
ral that the earlier steps in such a process should take the form of
unions of States most closely related by ties of common blood, lan-
guage, and institutions, and that a phase of federated Britain or Ang-
lo-Saxondom, Pan-Teutonism, Pan-Slavism, and Pan-Latinism
might supervene upon the phase already reached. There is perhaps
a suspicion of excessive logic in such an order of events, but a broad
general view of history renders it plausible and desirable enough.
Christendom thus laid out in a few great federal Empires, each with
a retinue of uncivilised dependencies, seems to many the most legit-
imate development of present tendencies, and one which would of-
fer the best hope of permanent peace on an assured basis of inter-
Imperialism. Dismissing from our mind the largest aspect of this
issue, as too distant for present profitable argument, and confining
our attention to British imperial federation, we may easily agree that
a voluntary federation of free British States, working peacefully for
the common safety and prosperity, is in itself eminently desirable,
and might indeed form a step towards a wider federation of civilised
States in the future.

The real issue for discussion is the feasibility of such a policy,
and, rightly stated, the question runs thus: "What forces of present
or prospective self-interest are operative to induce Great Britain and
her colonial groups to reverse the centrifugal process which has
hitherto been dominant?" Now, there are many reasons for Great
Britain to desire political federation with her self-governing colonies,
even upon terms which would give them a voice proportionate to
their population in a Parliament or other council charged with the
control of imperial affairs, provided the grave difficulties involved in
the establishment of such a representative, responsible governing
body could be overcome. The preponderance of British over colo-
nial population would enable the mother country to enforce her will
where any conflict of interest or judgment arose in which there was a
sharp line of division between Great Britain and the colonies: the
distribution of imperial burdens and the allocation of imperial assis-
tance would be determined by Great Britain. If the Crown colonies
and other non-self-governing parts of the Empire were represented
in the imperial council, the actual supremacy of the mother country
would be greater still, for these representatives, either nominated by
the Crown (the course most consonant with Crown colony govern-
ment), or elected on a narrow franchise of a small white oligarchy,
would have little in common with the representatives of self-govern-



IMPERIALISM



ing colonies, and would inevitably be more amenable to pressure
from the home Government. A chief avowed object of imperial fed-
eration is to secure from die colonies a fair share of men, ships, and
money for imperial defence, and for those expansive exploits which
in dieir initiation almost always rank as measures of defence. The
present financial basis of imperial defence is one which, on the face
of it, seems most unfair; Great Britain is called upon to support vir-
tually the whole cost of the imperial navy, and, with India, almost
the whole cost of the imperial army, though both these arms are at
the service of any of our self-governing colonies that is threatened by
external enemies or internal disorders. In 1899, while the popula-
tion of these colonies was close upon one-third of that of the United
Kingdom, their revenue nearly one-half, and the value of their sea-
borne commerce one-fifth of the entire commerce of the Empire,
the contribution they were making to the cost of the naval defence
of die Empire was less than one-hundredth part. 121 These colonies
raise no regular or irregular military force available for die general
defence of die Empire, though they have supported small contin-
gents of imperial troops quartered upon them by the Imperial Gov-
ernment, and have maintained considerable militia and volunteer
forces for home defence. The colonial contingents taking part in the
South African war, though forming a considerable volunteer force,
fell far short of an imperial lev}' based upon proportion of popula-
tion, and their expenses were almost entirely borne by the United
Kingdom. From the standpoint of die unity of die British Empire, in
which die colonies are presumed to have an interest equivalent to
that of die United Kingdom, it seems reasonable that the latter
should be called upon to bear their fair share of die burden of im-
perial defence; and an imperial federation which was a political reali-
ty would certainly imply a provision for such equal contribution.
Whatever were the form such federation took, diat of an Imperial
Parliament, endowed widi full responsibility for imperial affairs un-
der die Crown, or of an Imperial Council, on which colonial repre-
sentatives must sit to consult with and advise die British ministry,
who still retained die formal determination of imperial policy, it
would certainly imply a compulsory or quasi-compulsory contri-
bution on die part of die colonies proportionate to diat of die Unit-
ed Kingdom.

Now it is quite evident diat die self-governing colonies will not
enter such an association, involving diem in large new expenses, out
of sentimental regard for die British Empire. The genuineness and
the warmness of die attachment to die British Empire and to the
mother country are indisputable, and diough diey were not called
upon to make any considerable self-sacrifice in die Soudi African
campaign, it is quite evident diat dieir present sentiments are such as
would lead diem voluntarily to expend bodi blood and money
where diey diought die existence, the safety, or even die honour of



IMPERIALISM



the Empire was at stake. But it would be a grave error to suppose
that the blaze of enthusiastic loyalty, evinced at such a period of
emergency, can be utilised in order to reverse the general tendency
towards independence, and to "rush" the self-governing colonies
into a closer formal union with Great Britain, involving a regular
continuous sacrifice. If die colonies are to be induced to enter any
such association, they must be convinced that it is essential to their
individual security and prosperity. At present they get die protection
of die Empire without paying for it; as long as they think they can get
adequate protection on such terms, it is impossible to suppose they
would enter an arrangement which required them to pay, and which
involved an entire recasting of their system of revenue. The temper
of recent discussions in die Australian and Canadian Parliaments,
amid all die enthusiasm of die South African war, makes it quite
clear that no colonial ministry could in time of peace persuade the
colonists to enter such a federation as is here outlined, unless they
had been educated to die conviction that their individual colonial
welfare was to be subserved. Either Australia and Canada must be
convinced that imperial defence of Australia or Canada upon the
present basis is becoming more inadequate, and that such defence is
essential to them, or else they must be compensated for die addi-
tional expense which federation would involve by new commercial
relations with die United Kingdom which will give them a more
profitable market than they possess at present.

Now the refusal of die self-governing colonies hitherto to con-
sider any other contribution to imperial defence than a small volun-
tary one has been based upon a conviction that die virtual inde-
pendence they hold under Great Britain is not likely to be threat-
ened by any great Power, and that, even were it threatened, though
their commerce might suffer on die sea, they would be competent to
prevent or repel invasion by their own internal powers of self-
defence. The one exception to this calculation may be said to prove
die rule. If Canada were embroiled in war with her great republican
neighbour, she is well aware that though the British navy might
damage the trade and the coast towns of die United States, she
could not prevent Canada from being over-run by American troops,
and ultimately from being subjugated.

But, it may at least be urged, the importance of maintaining a
British navy adequate to protect their trade will at least be recog-
nised; die colonies will perceive that in face of die rising wealth and
naval preparations of rival Empires, in particular Germany, Erance,
and the United States, the United Kingdom cannot bear the finan-
cial strain of the necessary increase of ships without substantial colo-
nial assistance. This is doubtless the line of strongest pressure for
imperial federation. How far is it likely to prove effective? It is cer-
tain to educate colonial politicians to a closer consideration of the
future of their colony; it will force them to canvass most carefully the



IMPERIALISM



net advantages or disadvantages of the imperial connection. Such
consideration seems at least as likely to lead them towards that defi-
nite future severance from Great Britain which, during die last half-
century, none of diem has seriously contemplated, as it is to bring
diem into a federation. This consummation, if it ultimately conies
about, will arise from no abatement of natural good feeling and af-
fection towards the United Kingdom, but simply from a conflict of
interests.

If die movement towards imperial federation fails, and die re-
cent drift towards independence on die part of die self-governing
colonies is replaced by a more conscious movement in die same
direction, die cause will be Imperialism. A discreet colonial states-
man, when invited to bring his colony closer to Great Britain, and to
pay for dieir joint support while leaving to Great Britain die virtual
determination of dieir joint destiny, is likely to put die following per-
tinent questions: Why is Great Britain obliged to increase her ex-
penditure in armaments faster dian die growth of trade or income,
so that she is forced to call upon us to assist? Is it because she fears
die jealousy and die hostility of odier Powers? Why does she arouse
diese ill feelings? To these questions he can hardly fail to find an
answer. "It is die new Imperialism diat is wholly responsible for the
new perils of die Empire, and for the new costs of armaments." He
is dien likely to base upon diis answer furdier questions. Do we self-
governing colonies benefit by diis new Imperialism? If we decide
diat we do not, can we stop it by entering a federation in which our
voices will be die voices of a small minority? May it not be a safer
policy for us to seek severance from a Power which so visibly an-
tagonises odier Powers, and may involve us in conflict widi diem on
matters in which we have no vital interest and no determinant voice,
and eitiier to live an independent political life, incurring only those
risks which belong to us, or (in die case of Canada) to seek admis-
sion widiin die powerful republic of die United States?

However colonial history may answer diese questions, it is inevi-
table diat diey will be put. Imperialism is evidendy die most serious
obstacle to "imperial federation," so far as die self-governing colo-
nies are concerned. Were it not for die presence of these unfree
British possessions and for the expansive policy which continually
increases them, a federation of free British States diroughout the
world would seem a reasonable and a most desirable step in die in-
terests of world-civilisation. But how can die white democracies of
Australasia and Nordi America desire to enter such a hodge-podge
of contradictory systems as would be presented by an imperial fed-
eration, which might, according to a recent audiority, 122 be compiled
in die following fashion: first a union of Great Britain, Ireland, Can-
ada, West Indies, Australia, Tasmania, New Zealand, Newfound-
land, Mauritius, Soutii Africa, Malta, to be followed later by the ad-
mission of Cyprus, Ceylon, India, Hong-Kong, and Malaysia, with



IMPERIALISM



an accompaniment of semi-independent States such as Egypt, Af-
ghanistan, Natal, Bhutan, Jehore, and perhaps die kingdoms of
Uganda and of Barotse, each with some sort of representation on an
Imperial Council and some voice in die determination of die impe-
rial destiny?

Is it likely that die great rising Australian Commonwealth or die
Dominion of Canada will care to place her peaceful development
and her financial resources at die mercy of some Soudanese forward
movement or a pushful policy in West Africa?

An imperial federation comprising all sorts and conditions of
British States, colonies, protectorates, veiled protectorates, and
nondescripts, would be too unwieldy, and too prolific of frontier
questions and of other hazards, to please our more isolated and self-
centred free colonies; while, if these former were left without formal
representation as special proteges of die United Kingdom, their ex-
istence and dieir growth would none the less hang like a mill-stone
round the neck of die federal Government, constantly compelling
die United Kingdom to strain die allegiance of her confederates by
using her technical superiority of voting power in what she held to
be dieir special interest and hers.

The notion diat die absence of any real strong identity of interest
between die self-governing colonies and die more remote and more
hazardous fringes of die Empire can be compensated by some gen-
eral spirit of loyalty towards and pride in "die Empire" is a delusion
which will speedily be dispelled. The detached colonies of Australa-
sia may not unreasonably argue diat the very anxiety of British
statesmen to draw diem into federation is a confession of die weak-
ening of diat very protection which constitutes for diem die chief
value of the present connection. "The United Kingdom," they may
say, "asks us to supply men and ships and money in a binding en-
gagement in order to support her in carrying fartiier die very imperi-
alist policy which arouses die animosity of rival Powers and which
disables her for future reliance on her own resources to sustain the
Empire. For our increased contribution to the imperial resources we
shall dierefore receive in return an increase of peril. Is it not some-
tiling like asking us, out of pure chivalry, to dirow in our lot with a
sinking vessel?" It will doubdess be replied diat a firmly federated
Empire will prove such a tower of strengdi as will enable her to defy
die increased jealousy of rival Powers. But diis tempting proposition
will be submitted to cool calculation in our colonies, which will cer-
tainly refuse to be "rushed" into a change of policy implying a rever-
sal of the general tendency of half a century. Admitting die obvious
political and military gain of co-operative action in die face of an en-
emy, die colonists will ask whether diis gain is not offset by an in-
creased likelihood of having to face enemies, and when diey reflect
diat they are really invited to federate, not merely widi die England
whom diey love and admire, but widi an ever-growing medley of



IMPERIALISM



savage States, the balance of judgment seems likely to turn against
federation, unless other special inducements can be applied.

II

There are two special inducements which might bring the self-
governing colonies, or some of diem, to favour a closer political un-
ion widi Great Britain. The first is a revision of die commercial and
financial policy of die modier country, so as to secure for die colo-
nies an increased market for dieir produce in Great Britain and in
odier parts of die British Empire. In discussion of diis issue it is cus-
tomary to begin by distinguishing die proposal to establish an Impe-
rial Zollverein, or Customs Union, from die proposal for a preferen-
tial tariff. But very little reflection suffices to perceive the futility of
the former without die latter as an appeal to the self-interest of the
colonies. Will these colonies assimilate dieir financial policy to diat
of Great Britain, abolishing dieir protective tariffs and entering a full
Eree Trade career? The most sanguine Free Trader suggests no
such possibility, nor indeed would such a course afford any real
guarantee of increasing die commercial inter-dependence of the
Empire. It would simply force die colonies upon processes of direct
taxation repugnant to their feelings. Is Eree Trade within die Em-
pire, widi a maintenance of the status quo as regards foreign coun-
tries, really more feasible? It would simply mean diat die colonies
gave up the income they obtained from taxing die goods of one an-
other and of Great Britain, each getting in return a remission of tar-
iffs from the odier colonies widi which its trade is small and no re-
mission from Great Britain, which would continue to receive its
goods free as before. Though diis same policy would ultimately be
beneficial to dieir commerce, it would only encourage die existing
tendency to trade less widi die Empire and more widi foreign na-
tions, while it would involve a revolution of dieir fiscal mediod. No;
Eree Trade within die Empire is only conceivable upon die basis of
Great Britain agreeing to abandon Eree Trade widi countries out-
side die Empire. Even were Great Britain prepared to adopt such a
course, it would remain most unlikely diat die colonies would make
the sacrifice of customs income involved by admitting goods from all
die Empire free; for diis course would entail a sacrifice much greater
dian at first sight appears, inasmuch as such discrimination would
virtually enable die goods admitted free to displace the taxed goods,
reducing to quite insignificant dimensions the income still derived
from customs.

Engaged as we are now in finding special inducements to draw
die colonies into closer political union widi Great Britain, we need
not discuss die probability of an extension of die policy which Can-
ada initiated by her preferential tariff, according to Great Britain a



IMPERIALISM



preferential tariff by a surrender of duties upon British imports
amounting to 33 per cent. It is needless to discuss the motives which
ma)' have animated this advance. If we look to its result we find that
it has been quite inoperative, regarded as a stimulus to British trade.
"In spite of the preferential tariff, the percentage of American goods
entering Canada has continued to increase and the percentage of
British goods to decline." 125 This is attributed to the "sham" charac-
ter of die concession, as illustrated by the fact that "before giving a
preference to British goods the Laurier ministry was careful to raise
the duties on cotton goods largely coming from Great Britain, while
lowering or abolishing the duties on raw materials coming from the
United States." Thus the much-boasted British preference is to a
large extent a delusion. In spite of the preference, British goods still
pay a higher average tax on entering Canada than American goods.
